out you. Now kiss me, you pretty bitch, and make me like it!"
We now return to Helen, who picked up where we left off.
"I didn't hear any more, but I couldn't seem to go back to
sleep and after a while I heard a car drive into the yard and stop,
a door opened and somebody got out. They went around the back
way and got into the house. I listened closely and I heard them
come upstairs, pause a moment, and then cross to Mr. Van Revel's
door and knock lightly. Nobody answered so I'm sure that they
both must have been asleep. So the knock was repeated, but
louder. Then I heard my aunt, who must have awakened first and
called in a low voice:372
THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
"Who's there?"
"It was Mr. Elliott who answered, for I recognized his voice.
He said, 'It's Henry. Get up and come to the door. I didn't

snoring."
We now return to what young Helen overheard.
"Henry, what are you doing here?" The tone of Lizzie's
voice was very excited. She was frightened.
"What are you doing here?" Henry wanted to know, his eyes
blazing with anger.
"I work for Mr. Van Revel. You know that."
"And sleep with him, too?" Henry inquired, his angry eyes
flashing fire. Lizzie tried to avoid answering his question.
"Please, for heaven's sake, Henry, go away! You'll wake him

"I'll go, but you must come with me," said Henry.
"I can't. Please go away. I'll be home in the morning."
"I'm taking you with me now," Henry said, "right now!”
"Oh, Henry, why must you make trouble? You know what's
been going on, long before you came to Memphis."
"But you promised to cut it out; to give this white man and
all the niggahs too, up! You-"
"Henry, please listen to reason and go now, before you get us
both in trouble. I-"
At this point Van Revel, who had been snoring loudly, snorted,
became quiet, and then turning over, listened. Then he switched
on the light and looked at the spot where Lizzie had been lying.
Not seeing her, he felt of the spot as if to make sure, then thinking
that she might have got up to go to the bathroom, he turned over
on his side and started to go back to sleep again.
"Ssh!" Lizzie whispered, and laid her hand on Henry, turned
and cracked the door and looked over at Van Revel. The light
was still burning as he had not switched it off, and he must haveTHE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
373
heard her crack the door. He turned now and sat up, his eyes
on the door. She closed it quickly.
"Lizzie!" he called, loudly. "Is that you?"
Outside, Lizzie laid a hand on her lips and raised her finger
for silence. Elliott had grown angrier. He refused to be quiet
any longer. He shoved Lizzie aside so hard that she fell to the
floor and Van Revel, on hearing her, jumped out of bed, after
crying:
"What the hell's going on around here?"
He was wearing a nightgown, and in it he strode across to the
door, arriving there just as Elliott pushed it open and started in,
to meet Van Revel face to face. Van Revel paused, his eyes
flashed, his mouth flew open, but he was so shocked at the sight
of the angry Elliott that he stopped dead still, unable for a moment
to speak. Elliott glared at him.
"What the hell?" Van Revel finally managed to say, then looked
past Elliott to where Lizzie was rising from the floor. His mouth
still open in shocked surprise, he turned his eyes back to Elliott,
and found his voice again.
"Lizzie! What does this mean? This damned nigger here?"
"Oh, Mr. Van Revel!" Lizzie cried and started toward him.
Elliott with one quick blow, a sort of uppercut, floored her again,

her nightie, all that she had on, flying up around her shoulders,
exposing her entire body to her teaties. But neither man noticed
it, they were so intent on glaring at each other.
"Why, you goddamned black, scarfaced nigger, I-" Van Revel

he tumbled. For a moment he was stunned and lay there, breath-

THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD

Both managed to get to their feet now and stood up to face

Cleo had managed to stand up straight, and with perfect aim,
straight at Van Revel, pulled the trigger of his automatic twice,
the sound reverberating through the big house loudly. Lizzie
screamed, which woke the servants in the outside quarters, and
then Cleo turned the gun on her, as Van Revel sank back dying,
with the blood spurting from his mouth and nose and ears!
Seeming angrier than before, his eyes standing out like sharp
blue diamonds, Johnson aimed the gun straight at Lizzie and pulled
the trigger three times! Lizzie, with a final gasp, sank back upon
the pillow, dead!
Johnson looked around quickly, backed up, turned and looked
around him and listened. He could hear frightened Negroes down-
stairs and out in the yard around the house. He turned, found
the backstairs which he had come up a few minutes before, and
fled.

A
FTER BOB MARTIN dropped Walter Le Baron at the
Dolphin Hotel and drove on to his home on South Park-
way, Le Baron went into a huddle with himself. Accord-
ing to his calculations, if Cleo Johnson headed North toward St.
Louis and Chicago, and crossed over into Arkansas to do so, which
is the most popular auto bridge route north out of Memphis, then
he would have crossed the big bridge between three a. m. and five
a. m. that morning. The first thing to check on then would be to
find out what time the collectors of tolls at the bridge's entrance,
which was on the Tennessee side, changed watches.
He got into his car forthwith then, and drove out to the bridge,
got out and went up to the toll station where he was told that there
were three shifts; and that the man who came on at midnight
would be relieved at eight the following morning.
Returning to the hotel he purchased an evening paper, which
also carried a headline account of Van Revel's murder, and that
of Lizzie O'Neal, but in keeping with what had already appeared
in the morning paper, did not refer to her as Van Revel's concu-
bine, nor say that she had been found in bed with Van Revel when
the bodies were discovered. They did not even indicate in any
way that Van Revel was intimate with the woman. Much space
was devoted to the group of farmers who had organized a posse
and were scouring the hills in search of the killer, all of which
caused Walter Le Baron to smile.
It was with quiet satisfaction that Le Baron saw no account or
description of the murderer, further than to say that it was a
386THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
387
Negro. So Walter Le Baron sighed with his quiet satisfaction and
decided that since no one reported seeing Johnson, and there being
no description given of the killer in either of the Memphis papers,
he could inquire anywhere regarding Johnson without arousing sus-
picion. Thereupon he decided to return to the toll station at the
big bridge shortly after midnight and find out if Johnson had
crossed as he felt he had. While Johnson would have more than a
twenty-four hour start, since Le Baron did not plan to leave
Memphis until the following morning or even later, he knew that
he was at last on the heels of his man and could hope to catch up
with him somewhere, in St. Louis or Chicago, though most likely
in Chicago proper where Johnson would probably remain after he
arrived for an indefinite period.
That over, his plans laid, the balance of the afternoon and
evening were open, free and clear-and then he thought of Dor-

with her by telephone.
"Helle, darling," he said, after hearing her voice from the other
cnd.
"Hello," she called back, and seemed happy and excited on

I've been so anxious regarding you and your safety."
"Most busy, sweetheart. So busy that I couldn't even pause
long enough to call you."
"I thought that it was, perhaps, something like that, but I did
expect a call from you," she said, poutingly. "I stayed in the

Please accept an apology now, dear."

"Why, of course, Walter, dear. You didn't need to ask. You

389
"I'm ready, dear," Dorothy replied and looked up into his

was free to marry him as quickly as decency and respect would

With a little cry, she obeyed him, and both trembled from the

standing there and indulging in a great love forever, but Walter
Le Baron had a task to perform, a task which had already con-

efforts were possible of fruition very shortly, and regardless of
the happiness the free Dorothy was now giving him, standing

bone must break and snap, and then slowly and reluctantly he

sorrow which both had to give up and end this sweet bliss, was

now to close, and as he took her arm and started across the porch

"Dear Walter."

ALTER LE BARON called at the police department in
Memphis the following morning and after sending his
card in to the chief of police, was invited into that
one's office, and asked to be seated.
He promptly gave him a letter from Walton, police commis-
sioner for the City of New York, which caused the chief to open
his eyes in some surprise, after which he was shown far more
courtesy.
Calling in other officers on his staff, they questioned Le Baron
at considerable length, which he could see was more to try to get a
line on the killer, for by now the farmers who had organized them-
selves into a posse and gone into the hills to search, had reported
failure, and the Memphis police department hadn't anything to go
on, yet had declared to the newspapers that they would get the
Negro. The fact that the Van Revel family had posted a ten
thousand dollar reward, had the department anxious, and every-
body connected with it.
All they got out of Le Baron was that he was after the same
man, and wanted an agreement that the reward would be paid to
him for arrest and conviction of the killer, regardless where. Nat-
urally they insisted at the outset that he be returned to Memphis
for trial and conviction but Le Baron insisted just as strongly that
he could only promise them arrest and conviction.
Finally the chief called up the family and explained the cir-
cumstances and got their consent to Le Baron's demands and he
left as quickly as he received a letter signed by the chief of police,
402THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
403
to that effect. He left the office satisfied, but also left several anx-
ious and even jealous Memphis officers, who had a feeling that if
Van Revel's murderer was finally captured, it was most likely that
Walter Le Baron would be the man who would do it.
Le Baron went back to the hotel, paid his bill, checked out, got
into his car, drove by Dorothy's and kissed her and bid her good-
bye, then struck out for St. Louis, crossing the big bridge to the
Arkansas side, then turned northward for St. Louis, where he ar-
rived about nine o'clock that night.
Having been to St. Louis, he knew where all the hotels that
Negroes frequented were located. He drove around to several
before checking in, but was finally rewarded with the information
he was seeking at a hotel located at Jefferson Avenue and 26th
Street. He was advised that Johnson had spent the previous night
at that hotel, but had checked out early that morning. presumably
for Chicago, although he hadn't said where.
He wired Dorothy regarding the information he had secured,
and that he was spending the night in St. Louis and would drive
on through to Chicago the next day, and would wire her again on
arrival there, then he went to bed.
He enjoyed a good night's sleep, partook of a delightful break-
fast and then swung down the river a few blocks and crossed the
Free bridge across into East St. Louis, Illinois, turned left into
Highway 66, and headed for Chicago via Springfield where he
gassed, had lunch, made some inquiries, and smiled with satis-
faction when told in reply to his question, that Johnson had passed
through Springfield the day before. Furthermore, Johnson had had
lunch at the same place he dined, and left and drove north on 66,
which would be for Chicago as he suspected.
Le Baron knew that Johnson would not stop short of Chicago,
but reckoned that he might not be as easily found there, since the
Negro section of Chicago is about the largest from the standpoint
of square miles of any city in the United States, though New York
City has a larger Negro population. So after his arrival there, he
estimated that he might have to put in a lot of hard work, driving404
THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
around the Negro section before possibly picking up Johnson's
trail.
He put in the entire first day, following the night of his arrival,
driving from one hotel to another throughout the great Negro
section on the South Side, but failed to pick up any trace of John-
son. The next day he drove to the North Side and made a canvass
there, and from there to the West Side, which has a large colored
population, with the same results, no Johnson had been seen or
heard of anywhere! Yet he felt positive that Johnson was in the
city, and might have gotten wise that it would be safer to stop in
a private home; that the hotels might be canvassed by detectives
in a search for him, since the murder of Van Revel was something
that would perhaps bring about a long and concentrated search
for him.
Operating on a hunch, later, Le Baron started to contact pool
halls, hundreds of which were operating on the South Side, and he
knew it would take days to call on all of them, and if he failed to
pick up Johnson's trail at any, then it would be still harder for
him to get anywhere, seeking out crap-shooters and the thousands
of places they were likely to engage in it.
Three days went by and still he picked up no trace of John-
son. He decided to take an out-of-town flyer. Pickings might be
easier for Johnson in his profession in the towns around Chicago.
Accordingly, he drove to and canvassed Gary, Indiana, then drove
north, canvassed Evanston, then on to Milwaukee, Wisconsin-
still no trace of Cleo Johnson, easily recognizable by the scar on
his face, anywhere!
He began to get anxious about him. Where had the Negro
hidden himself?
He wrote Dorothy a long letter and told her of his thus far, ill
luck, but that he still felt sure that Johnson was in Chicago, and
that he would start on a new round of the pool halls, since Johnson
usually began his gambling operations from pool halls, where he
met men given to shooting craps and Le Baron had learned that
it was at pool halls that one could find out more about where toTHE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
405
go to shoot craps.
He had been in Chicago more than two weeks and had still
found no trace of Johnson, or where he was hanging out, and he
began to doubt himself. Came Sunday, and he called Dorothy by
telephone and talked with her five minutes, promising to wire or
even call her again as quickly as he picked up any clue regarding
Cleo.
He arose about ten a. m., and drove out to 47th Street and
had breakfast at Powers Restaurant on 47th Street, located just
east of the South Side elevated railroad station, where he often
dined during the times he had been in Chicago before. When one
dines at the front table, he can see the elevated trains going north
as they roar overhead from the South Side. While sitting there
idly, he overheard some couples talking about what a fine picture
they had seen at the Chicago Theatre, and, having nowhere to go
and with nothing in particular to do, he decided to drive downtown
and see it.
Usually, he would have turned his car around and driven back
to Chicago's South Parkway, and down it to the northern end of
the Loop, then turned left into Lake Street, then right at Wabash
Avenue into a parking lot, left his car, and then gone into the
theatre at State and Lake Streets.
Instead, Le Baron decided to drive down State Street, the
South end of which, especially from 22nd Street to 47th Street,
was a highway of Negro churches, most established since the de-
pression, in store front locations, and to count how many there
were in some of the blocks, where the most stores had been forced
out of business due to lack of trade.
He was amused, after counting sixteen in one block, and was
still amused and thinking about it when he reached and crossed
31st Street and was nearing 30th Street when he chanced to look
to his left and before him rose a pool hall that he had, in some
way, overlooked, but a pool hall which dated back to his early
youth, when as a boy he had arrived in Chicago and for the next406
THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
two years frequented it, although he had never indulged in playing
pool. As he passed it, and looked across the street into it, he
observed a large crowd of men, gathered around a table near the
center of the hall. When he reached and was crossing 30th Street,
it suddenly occurred to him to turn around and go back and look
to see why such a large crowd at that time of day, which was ear-
lier than players usually gather, at least on Sunday, in pool halls.
He was just in time to turn left at 29th Street before crossing
it, drive west and start to turn south again at old Dearborn Street,
but found it tied up with street repairing, so continued west to
Federal, once called Armour Avenue, where he turned left again,
drove through it, past an address at 3021 where he had lived for
more than a year when he first came to Chicago. He smiled rem-
iniscently, then turned left again at 31st Street and went back to
the old thoroughfare, turned left again and found himself back on
State Street. He drove on past the pool hall, made a U turn and
found himself in front of the old pool hall, but did not stop and
park for fear the car, with a New York license plate, might be
unduly noticed, but drove a couple of doors past it, and getting
out, walked back and entered the hall.
There were ten tables, including two billiard tables right up at
the front, all lined in a single row and reaching to the back of the
large store room perhaps sixty feet in depth.
He paused after closing the door and was relieved that his
entrance had not attracted any attention. The men, perhaps fifty
or more, were all gathered around a single table, some standing
on chairs to look over the heads of others, at a game of kelly pool
that was being played.
His curiousity was aroused and he strolled to the edge of the
crowd, and being over six feet tall, could catch a glimpse of the
game going on now and then, and of the players engaged, over the
heads of most of the men between him and the table, watching and
following the game with close interest.
Presently it was over and a medium sized man, whose back was
to Le Baron, seemed to have won for he was collecting money fromTHE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
407
the game keeper, who counted out quite a large number of one
dollar bills to him before the man with his back to him, moved.
He was naturally curious as were all present, to see the winner
of so many bills, but could not see his face until he should chance
to move.
"You're a pool shark, niggah," one of the men, obviously a
loser, complained, as he looked at the winner pocketing the roll
of bills.
"I was just lucky," the winner said.
"Lucky, hell! I tell you, fellows, this niggah is a pool shark,
and we's all acted the fool to play him and let him jes' steal ouh
money."

all." He paused and turned around a bit, and Le Baron started.
From what he could see of him, it looked like Cleo Johnson. His
heart beat faster, and his breath came shorter. He had on a top
coat, and he touched his sides where he carried a brace of pistols,
easy to get his hands on in a flash, but not where and so they
would attract any attention, otherwise.
"Well, let's start another game, so everybody who wants to
play or bet, get your money ready and lay your bets on the table,"
the winner said, and turned clear around---and Walter Le Baron
started! It was Cleo Johnson! Until he turned clear around, Le
Baron had been unable to see his full face, only the right side,
while the scar was on the left. He saw it in full now and for a
moment his heart was in his throat. He was at last face to face
with the killer, but who had not, up to then, noticed him.
"I've had enough of it," said one of the losers, and turning,
walked away from the table. One after another said the same
thing and Le Baron was getting on the anxious seat. It seemed
clear that Johnson had been hanging out at this pool hall all the
while; and that the players were beginning to get on to him, and
that for the present, there would not likely be another game. The
crowd were all leaving the table and Cleo Johnson standing there408
THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
by it, and Johnson again turned his back toward Le Baron and
shrugged his shoulders, and then turned-and looked straight at
Walter Le Baron! His eyes rested on Walter for two seconds, and
then his eyes narrowed, his suspicion was aroused!
Le Baron decided to get closer, so walked up nearer Johnson
who moved back and away a step. His eyes were mere slits now
and he paused and said:
"Say, who're you?"
"Oh," replied Le Baron, with feigned carelessness, "just a man
about town."
"You're a liar!" declared Johnson, backing up another step.
The crowd had stopped scattering and now turned to look on. They
felt that some kind of action was brewing; possibly a fight. All
present liked fights, and the way Johnson was looking at Le Baron,
they could all feel that something would start to happen-and
that it wouldn't be long in starting.
"What about another game?" Walter said, still pretending care-
lessness.
"You haven't told me who you are," all but hissed Johnson,
deeply suspicious. "I don't play with strangers! Tell me who
you are, and I'll reconsider."
"Oh, come on, Johnson, let's have another game! What does
my name matter?"
"Where'd you learn my name? Where, I say?"
He started a hand slowly toward his hip pocket, when Le
Baron, throwing off all pretense, cried:
"Hold it right there, Johnson! We have you covered!"
Immediately Johnson whirled to look around like the fright-
ened killer he was, and immediately he did so, Le Baron whipped
out a .45 automatic, and when Johnson turned his eyes back to
him, he looked straight into the muzzle of it! Le Baron was
smiling coldly.
"You're a goddamned flat foot, that's what you are!" declared
Cleo, and reached for his gun. There was a flash and a roar!
Le Baron had fired from the hip and the bullet tore through John-THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
409
son's coat sleeve and seared his wrist, which pained him, and he
swore loudly.
"Take it easy, Johnson and come with me peacefully, and that
will be all you get."
"Like hell I will!" Johnson cried, and then in a flash, reached
again, got his gun this time and fired, the shot going wild, and
Le Baron pulled the trigger twice, both bullets tearing Johnson's
coat sleeve but inflicting only painful flesh wounds as a result!
Johnson fired again, but Le Baron had ducked behind the end
of the pool table in the meantime. He was determined to take
Johnson alive if he could. He had never actually killed a man,
but realized if necessary to save himself, he might have to shoot
Johnson to kill.
Meanwhile, promptly the first shot was fired, Negroes who had
started to surround them, immediately-and hurriedly, ducked for
cover. Those near the front hurried outside in a drove, and fought
at the door while other shots were being fired, in an effort to get
out, screaming and arguing the while.
But those in the rear end of the hall were not so fortunate, for
the back door was locked, and the windows barred! There was
only the door that led to the basement and a foul toilet room be-
low, and after stampeding the rear door, breaking the windows and
jamming against the door, to find that escape via that route was
impossible. Thereupon, somebody started down through the toilet
room door and all made a break for it at the same time to get out.
Began to argue and greatly frightened, argued distractedly, calling
each other foul names and fighting at the same time to get out.
Meanwhile, let us return to Cleo Johnson and Walter Le Baron,
in a gun battle with each other across the pool table! The flash
and roar of their guns, punctuating and seeming to give action
and tense drama to the performance going on in the rear among
twenty to thirty scrambling and frightened Negroes, trying to
escape through a narrow toilet door.
Both men had emptied their guns by this time, each hoping
the other had only one-but not so! Both had a pair, and soon410
THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
reached for the second one. It was a tense situation, for both
were disturbed and annoyed by the cursing, and scrambling and
shrieking going on in the rear, and were tempted to look for a
moment at least in that direction, for if any had guns, they might
start shooting back there-which would be just too bad!
However, each evidently decided that he had to take that
chance, and to look after his own safety for the gun of the man
facing him, was uppermost. Accordingly, they continued to ex-
change shots at intervals, with Johnson hoping that he could get
Le Baron, and make his escape by the front way before outside
police help would come to Le Baron's assistance.
And while he was trying to think himself out of a desperate
situation, Le Baron was doing some thinking, too. As stated, his
ambition was to take Johnson alive, but if not-dead, of course.
But he was thinking fast, of a way to take him alive first.
He was squatting behind one end of the large and heavy table,
the bottom of which he could see reached within a foot of the floor
upon which it rested. He estimated that if he could fire a shot
under the table and strike Johnson in the foot, he might lose pos-
session of his gun long enough for him to rush forward and go
into action with him at close quarters. It was a long chance and
a risky one, but he knew that he had to do something before he
would have to reload, and which might give Cleo his chance.
Accordingly, he fired a shot across the table to detract atten-
tion and heard Johnson duck low to avoid taking it-and then in
a flash, Le Baron dropped on one knee, looked under the table,
saw Johnson's feet on the floor and the lower half of his buttocks
and knew that he was squatting. Taking careful aim, he pulled
the trigger, the gun barked and the bullet went straight into John-
son's foot and he screamed, fell over, lost possession of his re-
volver, which Le Baron heard slide away, and he bounded to his
feet like a tiger, rushed around the table and jumped on top of
Johnson who was writhing in pain, overpowered him for a moment
and got the handcuffs on one wrist before Johnson could recover,
and realize what was going on! But before Le Baron could getTHE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
411
it on the other, Johnson went into action with him. They fought
and struggled all around the table, and Le Baron was surprised at
the strength and fortitude Johnson demonstrated. From the be-
ginning, he knew that he would master Johnson, and had been
trained by his father years before in how to do so. Finally, just
before police officers arrived, he got the hold on Johnson that did
it and slowly but surely he forced him into a position where he
could no longer resist, Le Baron brought Johnson's other wrist, all
bloody and hard to hold, close enough to the other to get the cuff
around. A moment later he heard it snap, and knew that he had
captured Cleo Johnson, and that he would never have a chance
to kill or rob another person.
Le Baron, with the arrival of the officers, turned him over to
them, pulled his coat back, displayed his badge, told them as best
he could to hold the other while he caught his breath and sat down
on a chair until he had done so.
When he told them what Johnson was wanted for, including
the murder of Van Revel and Lizzie O'Neal, recently in Memphis,
they hustled him off, first to the 27th and State police station. A
few minutes after he had been booked there, they put him in a
barred wagon and drove him on to the North Side Jail's hospital
where his wounds were dressed and he was booked as a fugitive
and murderer, a charge that puts a prisoner behind the strongest
bars available, and which is where Johnson was put.
"So he's the killer that's had Memphis in a dither for days, eh?"
the Lieutenant in charge ventured as Le Baron got into his car
preparatory to driving back to his hotel on the South Side.
"He's the man," Le Baron replied with a tired smile, glad that
it was all over and anxious to get to his room for a bath and some
much needed rest.
"Well, you're a lucky man is all I can say," the Lieutenant
went on. "Every police officer the country over has been on the
lookout for him. If they had known he had such a scar on his
face as he is wearing, somebody might have beat you to him," and
the officer winked and smiled as he said this. Le Baron just smiled412
THE STORY OF DOROTHY StanFIELD
back at him as he drove away and headed for the South Side.
Le Baron had also suffered some injuries which had to be dres-
sed before he left the jail's hospital. On his way back to the
South Side, he stopped by the main office of the Western Union
Telegraph Company, in downtown Chicago and sent Dorothy the
following telegram:
DOROTHY STANFIELD
1385 SOUTH PARKWAY
MEMPHIS, TENN.
CAPTURED CLEO JOHNSON THIS NOON IN A POOL
HALL AFTER A SENSATIONAL GUN BATTLE AND
HAND TO HAND STRUGGLE. HE IS IN CUSTODY
AT THE NORTH SIDE POLICE HOSPITAL. LETTER
FOLLOWING. LOVE.
WALTER
When Dorothy Stanfield left home for church, where she had
always gone on Sundays, except when she drove out of the city,
she was not expecting a message and was therefore glad to leave
home and enjoy a good sermon which she heard preached at the
First Congregational Church that morning.
Imagine her surprise when she opened her front door on re-
turning home, and there, having been pushed under it, was the
familiar telegram which by now she had grown accustomed to
receiving. She started, and her heart beat faster, for after Le
Baron had called and talked with her by long distance, that morn-
ing, she hadn't expected to hear from him any more that day, so
knew that the telegram meant that something unusual had hap-
pened! As she opened it, her fingers trembled and she became
possessed with a great fear.
"Oh, Lord, I hope nothing has happened to him," she whis-
pered to herself, and held her breath until she got it open, unfolded
it, spread it out and read it.
After she had done so, however, she staggered across the front
room and sank limp onto the sofa and lay down, she was thatTHE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
413
happy! She read the telegram over and over again to be sure
that it meant what it said, and that her anxiety over the safety
of the man she loved, was at an end, and she could relax at last
into the calm and peace of their romance and await the day when
he would return to Memphis, or when he would send for her, and
they could be happy.
She wrote a telegram to him, rewrote it several times, then
called it to the Western Union Office over the telephone, and retired
to rest and think of him without anything to restrain her great love
any longer.
Dorothy Stanfield was so greatly relieved and happy at the
sudden turn of events, that she remained in bed for many hours
and continued to think of Walter Le Baron. The last barrier to
their marriage and future happiness had been removed by the cap-
ture and arrest of the desperado, Cleo Johnson in Chicago, whom
Le Baron had trailed over most of the South. First, out of the
North into the South, then out of the South back into the North
again. But because of which, however, she might have never met
him in the gas station on Beale Street that Sunday morning. It
was all, as she looked back on it, so strange, so unusual!
As she continued to relive her past with Stanfield, she recalled
when and how unhappiness and disappointment began on their
honeymoon trip to Chicago! Never had she been able to erase
that trip-and of his almost insane performance that first night in
a Pullman berth on their way North! She recalled how, on their
arrival in Chicago and after registering in at the Vincennes hotel,
that the first thing she did was to sit down and write her parents,
begging them in a long letter, to come and get her, and to apply
for an annulment of the marriage forthwith! Had they done as
she asked them to, years of bitter misery and unhappiness that she
later suffered, could not have happened.
Nevertheless, she had never faulted them for persuading her
to try it longer. Then came his first arrest and disgrace-charged
with the crime of abortion! The trial then, and of his acquittal,
due in a large share to his smart, Jewish lawyer, Leo Bernstein;414
THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
and to the fact of the Judge not being inclined to take the murder
of a colored girl very seriously!
Followed then, two years of mysterious prosperity, climaxed by
his second arrest for murder and swindling! Into the picture then
again came Bernstein. After due consideration, he told him he was
as good as convicted, and that they might hang him this time, to
rid Memphis of what was substantially a nuisance!
But Doctor Stanfield was a resolute man. In due time he per-
suaded Bernstein that he could be cleared of the charge - and
showed him how!
Doctor Stanfield told him that Max Goldstein, of New York,
which doesn't happen to be his real name, could do it. Bernstein
contacted Goldstein by long distance. Goldstein consented to come
to Memphis, if a retainer of $10,000 was posted. Doctor Stanfield,
as we know, was almost rich through the success of his swindling,
so he agreed and posted it.
Dorothy Stanfield, as she continued to relive her past life, re-
called Max Goldstein's summation! Never, if she lived to be one
hundred years old, would she ever forget that summation! The
man simply paralyzed the court's imagination; the jury, the judge
--herself even! He left everybody stupefied and while in this
state of stupefaction, the jury brought in a verdict of "not guilty"
---when Nathan Dent Stanfield, she knew, and everybody who had
heard the trial, knew, too-was as guilty as hell!
Bob Martin, as we have heard, told Walter Le Baron what
happened, as Doctor Stanfield started out of the court a free man,
a circumstance and a fact that not even Max Goldstein had reck-
oned, and which was beyond Max Goldstein's magnetic powers
even, to change. So in spite of all efforts to free him, he was sent
to prison, where, as we already know, he died.
"Now it is all over," she sighed to herself, as she turned over
and started to thinking of it all over again. "I am free, in love,
and will soon marry the dearest man in all the world!"
Le Baron stayed in Chicago until Cleo Johnson was well enoughTHE STORY OF DOROTHY StanFIELD
415
to be returned to New York, although the state of Tennessee fought
hard to get him extradited to Memphis, where he would have been
hanged much quicker than he could be executed legally in New
York. But in due time Le Baron managed to settle it and satisfy
both states and Johnson was returned to New York, where a few
weeks later he went on trial for the murder of Isadore Zacarrio,
was promptly found guilty, convicted, and day of execution duly
set. He was due to be executed in ninety days at Sing Sing Prison,
which conviction was sufficient for Walter Le Baron to collect all
the rewards that had been offered for his arrest and conviction,
amounting to almost thirty thousand dollars.
Almost four months had gone by since he had seen Dorothy
and he decided that four months was long enough to wait, but
didn't inform Dorothy that he thought so. Instead, he quietly
took the Memphis Special one day and two days later, unannounced,
he knocked quietly on her door on South Parkway and when she
opened it, she almost fainted!
Later, inside the house, with his arm about her waist, he said:
"We've waited quite long enough, Dorothy dear. We love each
other too much to wait any longer, so I have returned to Memphis
to make you my own dear wife just as quickly as it can be ar-
ranged." As he finished, she looked at him in happy surprise,
and then melting, replied:
"I've always said, after you met me and we fell in love all in
one day, dear Walter, that I could never refuse you anything.
Now what can I do, and what do you want me to do?"
"Get ready to marry me just as quickly as you can, after which
we will leave immediately for New York by plane, and within one
week, thereafter, we will sail for a trip around the world!"
"Oh, Walter, darling, do you honestly mean it?"
He looked at her and smiling, drew her closer.
"Dorothy, my own Dorothy, I have been waiting for this hour
all my life, the hour to lead to the altar the girl of my dreams.
To marry a normal man who will love, honor and respect you,416
THE STORY OF DOROTHY STANFIELD
has been your dream, too, hasn't it, Dorothy?"
"My dream, Walter, all my life."
"We are now at the crossroads of life and let us hope that it
will be a long life and a happy life. Good fortune has favored
us to make the dreams of both possible. I have cancelled all de-
mands that might be offered for my services for six months, darl-
ing, during which I am going to devote all the time to travel, and
to loving the sweetest girl in all the world. That is all I want,
Dorothy, and what I ask of you."
"Oh, my darling, this is all too wonderful for words; too won-
derful to even think about! Please say again, my Walter, that it
is true, and then I will be as you wish, my precious, yours
forever!"
-
And thus ends this story of a brave, resolute and courageous
man, and a kind, tender, faithful, affectionate and beautiful woman.
